You're a student learning to understand and draw one kind of animal beautifully in painterly pixel art, for voxsim, a simulation of Washington State around 1700: **black bear and cougar, the two big predators of Washington's forests**.

Both are high on voxsim's list of animals that still draw as stand-ins: black bear as the old brown bear, cougar as a short-tailed lynx. A bear fishing a salmon run, or a cougar on a ledge above deer, are the kind of moments the sim should be able to show. The work covers what makes each unmistakable (the bear's shoulder hump-less profile and rolling gait; the cougar's long heavy tail, small head and low stalk), their gaits and postures, and their colours by season and age.

**The gold standard.** The studio's elk and deer student produced some of the best images of the whole project: a grazing Roosevelt elk herd, and a galloping bull that reads as an elk and is alive in motion at every size. Before anything else, read its art school post closely:

- ~/work/artschool/posts/deer-elk/TUTORIAL.md and POST.md;
- the seven how-tos in howto/;
- its notebook in ~/work/artschool/notebooks/deer-elk/;
- its code in ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/deer-elk/src/.

What made it work, in its own words:

- **It sourced very detailed references before drawing.** It read the naturalist's guides completely, found Muybridge's own frame-by-frame plates, measured proportions from aligned photos, and followed the "four things that make a 6-pixel elk read".
- **It built a comparison board first:** photo | render at the same shoulder height, on the same ground line, in black silhouette (notan), then both area-reduced to the sizes the animal is actually seen at. Every big correction came off that board.
- **It rigged in 3-D for sweeping poses, facings and gaits, and painted in 2-D** on a material × shade-step index canvas, with palette as light.
- **It recognised that small figures need a different painter.** Below ~10 px it stopped rendering and placed pixels: body, legs, head, the one high-contrast patch.
- **It did hero size and small sizes together throughout,** and put the animals into other students' paintings in the right light and scale.
- **It debugged by printing the actual numbers and characters,** not by guessing.

You don't have to copy its approach; your animal may need a different one. But that is the bar, and that is the kind of care.

**Find your subject's model system first.** Before drawing, find the expert system that already explains how your subject looks and works, and build on it. The elk student built on Muybridge's plates and the naturalist's "four things". Natural scientists used to be trained to draw: a geologist could draw a flake, an outcrop or a fold with economy and truth. Naturalist painting guides were built on the same science. The studio library, ~/work/pixelart-studies/library/README.md, collects them:

- Lobeck's and Davis's block diagrams and Imhof's rock drawing for terrain;
- Ruskin and Holmes's Gran

# Painting elk and deer at every size

*By the elk and deer student. The story of how I learned this, dead ends included, is in [POST.md](POST.md); my raw working thread is [the notebook](../../notebooks/deer-elk/notebook.md).*

![the dusk gravel bar](img/dusk_gravel_bar_4x.png)

*Roosevelt elk on the channel student's dusk gravel bar: a bull on the bar, cows scattered behind, one wading. Painted by the study's code (`context.py`) onto another student's painting.*

My subject was Roosevelt elk and black-tailed deer, as individuals and in herds, from a 60 px hero animal down to a 2 px dot in a herd seen from the air, in any facing and gait, at any hour, in the landscape view and the 30° iso view. What I have to teach isn't one technique, and it isn't mainly Ferrari's. The one thing that made my animals stop reading as horses was an instrument: **a photo and my render side by side, same shoulder height, same ground line, in black silhouette, then shrunk to the size it will actually be seen.** Most of the rest is what that instrument taught me, organised so you can use it without spending the night I spent.

So these pages are seven how-tos, in the order I'd do them now. Each is taught from the real code in my study folder, `~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/deer-elk/src/`, and from the real images, either ones I looked at while working ("nb", numbered as in the notebook) or re-renders of the study's own code made for these pages by [`howto/figs.py`](howto/figs.py) ("fig"). Nothing here was repainted with a simpler painter.

# 1. The board: photo against render, in silhouette and at size

*[Back to the tutorial](../TUTORIAL.md). Code: `board.py`, `sizes.py`, `sizes_c.py`, `cmp_photo.py` in `~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/deer-elk/src/`.*

Every correction that made my elk stop reading as a horse came off one instrument. I'd have got there an hour sooner if I'd built it before the first render instead of after, so it's first here.

It has three parts:

1. **the notan board**: photo | render at the same shoulder height, standing on the same ground line, the render as a black silhouette;
2. **the grey and colour boards**: the same layout, once the silhouette is right;
3. **the size board**: the photo *area-reduced* to 60, 30, 16 and 8 px of body length, beside the render at those sizes.

## Choosing the photos

You need side views, or near side views, of real animals standing on visible ground, with the whole animal in frame. From the naturalist's sheets and my own Openverse fetches (`python3 ~/work/pixelart-studies/tools/imgfetch.py "roosevelt elk cow" -n 10 -o refs/roosevelt_elk_cow --sheet`), I used four:

| photo | why |
|---|---|
| a Roosevelt cow standing alert, her calf bedded at her feet | the cow's neck carriage and body length; a real bedded calf |
| "Majestic Bull", a Roosevelt bull side-on in autumn | the mane, the orange body, antlers from the side |
| Muybridge, *Animal Locomotion* plate 692, frame 15: an elk trotting | a gait, drawn from life, on a measured ground line |
| a Columbian blacktail buck walking | the deer's proportions and ears |

The searches worth making were specific: "roosevelt elk cow", "elk trotting" and "muybridge elk" (which returned Muybridge's own plates) were gold. Generic ones ("elk bedded down") returned half junk.

# The studio library: model systems

The elk and deer student succeeded largely because it didn't start from raw photos. It started from finished analysis:

- Muybridge's frame-by-frame plates;
- the naturalist's "four things that make a 6-pixel elk read".

Natural scientists used to be trained to draw: a geologist could draw a flake, an outcrop or a fold with economy and truth. Naturalist painting guides were built on the same scientific understanding. This library collects those traditions so studies can start where the experts finished, instead of doing a geology PhD from photographs.

**How to use it:**

- Before drawing, find your subject's model system here (or online, below) and read it closely.
- Then build on it, the way the elk student built on Muybridge.
- Open the PDFs and look at the plates; the drawings are the point.

Refetch anything missing with `./fetch.sh` (resumable). Files are also mirrored in S3 under `voxsim/shared/library/`.

## On disk (open access)

| Domain | File | What it gives you |
|---|---|---|
| Terrain, landforms | `terrain/blockdiagramsoth0000armi_s8g3.pdf` | **Lobeck, *Block Diagrams* (1924).** How geologists draw landforms in 3-D: every landform type as a block diagram, with the drawing method. |
| Terrain, landforms | `terrain/2015.205632.Geomorphology.pdf` | **Lobeck, *Geomorphology* (1939).** Landforms explained through his own drawings: glacial troughs, cirques, rivers, coasts, volcanoes. |
| Terrain, landforms | `terrain/physicalgeograp00davigoog.pdf` | **W. M. Davis, *Physical Geography* (1898).** Davis's block diagrams: how landscapes form and age. |
| Terrain, rock at map scale | `terrain/2015.119840.Cartographic-Relief-Presentation.pdf` | **Imhof, *Cartographic Relief Presentation*.** The rigorous method for showing terrain by light, and Swiss rock drawing: cliffs rendered by structure with a few strokes. Probably the single most useful text for rock at landscape and region scale. |
| Rock, mountains | `rock/modernpainters04rusk.pdf` | **Ruskin, *Modern Painters IV: Of Mountain Beauty* (1856).** A painter's analysis of rock types, cleavage, crests, banks and stones, written for artists and illustrated with his own drawings. |
| Rock, strata at distance | `rock/holmes/*.jpg` | **William Henry Holmes, plates for the Grand Cañon Atlas (1882).** The masterpieces of American scientific landscape drawing: stratified cliffs and plateaus at great

36:- **Black bear:** **rump higher than the shoulders**, back sloping forward and down; a **straight face with prominent ears**; short, thick legs; a rounded barrel. No hump (a hump means grizzly).
37:- **Cougar:** long, low and heavy-hindquartered; a small round head; **a tail as long as half the body, carried low with an up-curl at the tip.** When stalking, **the head is below the shoulder blades**, which stand up like two peaks.
40:- **Mountain goat:** **blocky, deep-chested, humped at the shoulder, narrow-hipped**, head carried low; beard and "pantaloons" hang down the front legs. Like a small white bison.
56:| **bedded / resting** | legs folded under, body a low oval, head up or turned back onto the flank | deer bed in cover; elk bed in groups in forest; bears sprawl; marmots sprawl on rocks |
59:| **stalking predator** | **belly low, head below the shoulder line, ears forward, slow deliberate steps**, freezes | cougar; coyote before a pounce |
61:| **curious bear** | **standing up on its hind legs** to see or smell (not aggression) | |
62:| **threatened bear** | head low, ears back, huffing, jaw popping, swatting the ground; bluff charge = a short rush that stops | |
66:| **dead** | carcass on its side, legs stiffly out; a cougar kill is **cached under raked duff** with a leg sticking out | |

# Predators: cougar, black bear, coyote, bobcat (and wolf, grizzly, lynx, fisher)

*Animal ecology, naturalists' studio, 2026-09-25. Sources at the end, cited inline in [brackets]. "Field knowledge" = standard carnivore biology I am confident of but have not tied here to a Washington-specific paper; treat those numbers as ±30%.*

## The one-paragraph version

West of the Cascades today, the large predators are **cougar** (rare, ~2 per 100 km², the main killer of deer and adult elk), **black bear** (common, ~12–31 per 100 km², mostly a vegetarian that kills fawns and elk calves for a few weeks each June and eats salmon in autumn), **coyote** (a 20th-century arrival on the Olympics, eating voles, fawns, fruit and marmots) and **bobcat** (hares, mountain beavers, squirrels). **Before contact the Olympic Peninsula had wolves and no coyotes.** East of the crest, wolves are back (270 in 49 packs at the end of 2025). Grizzlies are functionally absent statewide; Canada lynx hang on in the high Okanogan; fishers have been reintroduced to the Olympics and Cascades.

---

## Part A. For the sim builders

### A1. Numbers per species

| | mass | home range | density (per 100 km²) | activity | main prey / food |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Cougar** *Puma concolor* | males ~55–75 kg, females ~35–50 kg; 2.0–2.4 m nose to tail tip, tail ~0.8 m [field knowledge] | **W WA: females 50–65 mi² (130–170 km²), males ~150 mi² (~390 km²)** [Kertson et al. 2011, via WDFW/press] | **1.55–2.79 independent cougars** across 5 WA study areas, median 2.34 [Beausoleil et al. 2021] | nocturnal–crepuscular | deer, elk (esp. calves), also beaver, raccoon, coyote, hares |

"""The animal as a 3-D rig: species proportions, pose, gait -> a list of tubes.

Units are shoulder heights (H = 1). Axes: x forward, y left, z up; the animal stands on z = 0
with its withers near x = 0.3.

A tube is a centreline of stations, each with an elliptical cross-section (a along the local
"up" normal, b along the lateral binormal), a material function, and a flag for thin parts
(lower legs, ears, antlers, tail) that the pixel painter redraws as clean runs when they get
narrower than a pixel or two.

The torso is not a capsule. It is lofted from stations read off side-view photos and the
Muybridge plates: the level back with the withers a touch above the croup, the deep chest, the
belly rising into the flank tuck, the ham dropping behind it and the buttock. That profile is
most of what separates a cervid from the horse the old capsule rig drew.
"""
import numpy as np
from dataclasses import dataclass, field, replace

# material ids (hide regions). The painter maps each to a ramp.
BODY, DARK, PALE, BELLY, LEG, HOOF, ANTLER, TIP, NOSE, EARIN, THROAT, TAILTOP, SPOT, VELVET, EYE, FACE = range(16)
MAT_NAMES = ["body", "dark", "pale", "belly", "leg", "hoof", "antler", "tip", "nose", "earin",
             "throat", "tailtop", "spot", "velvet", "eye", "face"]


@dataclass
class Tube:
    name: str
    c: np.ndarray        # (n,3) centreline
    a: np.ndarray        # (n,) radius along up-normal
    b: np.ndarray        # (n,) radius along lateral
    up: np.ndarray       # (3,) reference up for framing
    mat: object          # int or fn(u in 0..1 along, theta, pts) -> ids
    thin: bool = False
    cap0: bool = True
    cap1: bool = True
    width_px_min: float = 1.0   # when drawn as a line, never thinner than this


# ---------------------------------------------------------------- species

@dataclass
class Species:
    name: str
    H: float                     # shoulder height in metres
    torso: list                  # stations (x, ztop, zbot, halfwidth)
...

# output:
"""Tubes -> supersampled surface splats -> pixel index canvas (material, step).

The pipeline:

  1. sample every tube's surface densely (points closer than half a supersample pixel),
     with normals and a hide material per point (coat.py);
  2. project orthographically for a camera yaw and elevation (0 = side view, 30 = iso);
     z-buffer the splats; a light-space z-buffer gives self- and ground-shadow;
  3. reduce each ss x ss block to one pixel: coverage, mean normal, majority material, depth;
  4. thin parts (lower legs, ears, antlers, tail) get their centrelines redrawn as clean runs
     so they never break up at small sizes;
  5. form classes (light 5, 4 | halftone 3 || reflected 2 | shadow 1 | core 0) from the lit
     normal, the shadow test, bounce from the ground and the sky.
"""
import numpy as np
from dataclasses import dataclass
import coat

STEPS = 6
SHADOW_REACH = 0.5   # cast shadow kept within this many body-lengths of the body (herd scenes raise it)


@dataclass
class View:
    yaw: float = 0.0        # degrees; 0 = animal faces screen-right, side on
    elev: float = 8.0       # camera elevation in degrees (0 side, 30 iso)
    sun_az: float = -130.0   # sun azimuth in the world, degrees (0 = screen right, 90 = away from viewer)
    sun_el: float = 40.0
    ss: int = 6             # supersampling
    diffuse: float = 0.0    # 0 = direct sun; 1 = overcast (sky light from above, no cast shadow)


def sun_vec(az, el):
    a, e = np.radians(az), np.radians(el)
    return np.array([np.cos(e) * np.cos(a), np.cos(e) * np.sin(a), np.sin(e)])


def _frames(c, up):
    t = np.gradient(c, axis=0)
    t /= np.linalg.norm(t, axis=1, keepdims=True) + 1e-12
    u = np.broadcast_to(up, c.shape).astype(float)
    n1 = u - (u * t).sum(1, keepdims=True) * t
    bad = np.linalg.norm(n1, axis=1) < 1e-4
    if bad.any():
        alt = np.array([0, 0, 1.0]) if abs(up[2]) < 0.9 else np.array([1.0, 0, 0])
        n1[bad] = alt - (alt * t[bad]).sum(1, keepdims=True) * t[bad]
    n1 /= np.linalg.norm(n1, axis=1, keepdims=True) + 1e-12
    n2 = np.cross(t, n1)
    return t, n1, n2


def sample_tube(tb, ds):
    """Points, normals, u (0..1 along), cos, sin on the tube surface at spacing ds (metres)."""
    c, a, b = tb.c, tb.a, tb.b
    seg = np.linalg.norm(np.diff(c, axis=0), axis=1)
    s = np.concatenate([[0], np.cumsum(seg)])
    Ltot = s[-1]
    prof = np.abs(np.diff(a)).sum() + np.abs(np.diff(b)).sum()
    n_al = max(int(np.ceil((Ltot + 2 * prof) / ds)) + 1, 2)
    t = np.linspace(0, Ltot, n_al)
    if len(s) >= 3 and np.all(np.diff(s) > 1e-9):
        from scipy.interpolate import PchipInterpolator
        C = PchipInterpolator(s, c, axis=0)(t)
        A = PchipInterpolator(s, a)(t)
        B = PchipInterpolator(s, b)(t)
    else:
        C = np.stack([np.interp(t, s, c[:, k]) for k in range(3)], 1)
        A = np.interp(t, s, a)
        B = np.interp(t, s, b)
    U = t / max(Ltot, 1e-9)
    T, N1, N2 = _frames(C, tb.up)
    per = 2 * np.pi * np.s

"""Hide regions: which material each surface point wears.

Roosevelt elk: warm tan body; dark chocolate head, neck, mane, brisket, belly and legs; a pale
straw rump patch edged dark; a small tail the colour of the patch. Cows the same pattern, less
contrast. Calves: red-brown with cream spots in rows along the back.

Black-tailed deer: grey-brown (winter) or rusty (summer) all over, paler belly and inner legs, a
white throat patch, a dark forehead on bucks, a black nose, big ears with pale insides and dark
rims, a broad tail black on top and white beneath (the white shows when it is flagged).
"""
import numpy as np
from rig import (BODY, DARK, PALE, BELLY, LEG, HOOF, ANTLER, TIP, NOSE, EARIN, THROAT, TAILTOP,
                 SPOT, VELVET, EYE, FACE)

NMAT = 16
PRIORITY = [NOSE, EYE, TAILTOP, SPOT]


def _hash(a, b, seed):
    h = (np.floor(a).astype(np.int64) * 73856093) ^ (np.floor(b).astype(np.int64) * 19349663) ^ (seed * 83492791)
    return ((h % 1000003) / 1000003.0 + 1) % 1.0


def spots(x, cth, sth, seed, xr, xf, rows=((0.74, 0.0), (0.46, 0.5), (0.16, 0.2)), dx=0.10, r=0.022):
    """Fawn and calf spots: dots in rows along the back, each row offset half a step, spaced
    ~0.075 H, radius ~0.022 H; a little jitter per dot (deterministic in seed)."""
    ang = np.arctan2(sth, cth)                 # 0 at the spine top, +-pi at the belly
    out = np.zeros(len(x), bool)
    for k, (cl, off) in enumerate(rows):
        a0 = np.arccos(np.clip(cl, -1, 1))      # the row's angle from the spine
        for sgn in (1, -1):
            along = (x - xr) / dx + off
            i = np.round(along)
            jit = (_hash(i, k * 7 + (sgn > 0), seed) - 0.5) * 0.5
            d_al = (along - i - jit) * dx
            d_ac = (np.abs(ang) - a0) * 0.18       # arc length ~ angle x radius (~0.18 H)
            hit = (d_al ** 2 + d_ac ** 2 < r * r) & (np.sign(ang + 1e-9) == sgn) & (x > xr + 0.1) & (x < xf - 0.14)
            hit &= _hash(i, k * 13 + (sgn > 0), seed + 1) < 0.7
            out |= hit
    return out


def materials(ind, tb, p, n, u, cth, sth):
    sp = ind.spec()

    q = p / H
    nm = tb.name
    elk = ind.species == "elk"
    young = ind.age == "young"
    out = np.full(len(p), BODY, np.int32)
    if nm == "torso":

        uu = np.linspace(0, 1, len(tb.xs))
        x = np.interp(u, uu, tb.xs)
        zl = np.interp(u, uu, tb.zc) + np.interp(u, uu, tb.ra) * cth
        up = cth
        xr = sp.torso[-1][0]      # rear end x
        xf = sp.torso[0][0]
        if elk:
            out[:] = BODY
            # the cape: the dark neck colour runs back over the shoulder on a diagonal, from just
            # behind the withers down to behind the elbow, and covers the brisket
            cape_x = 0.19 + (1.0 - zl) * 0.34 + (0.03 if ind.sex == "m" else 0.0)
            out[x > cape_x] = DARK
            out[(zl < np.interp(x, [xr, xf], [0.66, 0.56]) + 0.05) & (x

"""The small painter (under ~14 px of body length): a designed glyph from the projected skeleton.

At these sizes the splat renderer turns legs, neck and antlers into blobs (stages/s1/
ladder_micro1). A pixel artist at this scale places pixels, so this painter does too, from
keypoints of the same rig:

  body   per column, the torso's projected top and bottom, rounded; at least 2 rows (>= 6 px)
         and never more rows than it has columns / 2.5; corners knocked off at >= 9 px
  zones  elk: the rear ~18% of the body is the pale rump, the front ~28% the dark cape;
         deer and calves: one colour. Top row in the sun, the rest one step down.
  legs   per pair (fore, hind), the near leg as a clean 1-px run from the belly to the hoof;
         the far leg only when its hoof is 2+ px away from the near one (walking, running),
         one step darker
  neck   base -> poll, 1 px (a bull's mane makes it 2 at >= 10 px)
  head   poll -> nose, 1 px; below 7 px just the poll and the nose
  antler bull elk: the beam as a 1-px run rising back from the poll (>= 8 px); a single dark
         pixel above the head below that; tines at >= 12 px
  ears   deer: a 1-px tick up from the poll at >= 8 px
The value plan is the far read from the naturalist's guide: dark front, light back, the pale
rump pixel, the head up or down.
"""
import numpy as np
import dataclasses
import rig, render
from render import clean_line, view_xf


def _put(mat, step, part, x, y, m, s, p, overwrite=True):
    H, W = mat.shape
    if 0 <= x < W and 0 <= y < H and (overwrite or mat[y, x] < 0):
        mat[y, x] = m
        step[y, x] = s
        part[y, x] = p


def _line(mat, step, part, a, b, m, s, p, overwrite=True):
    for (x, y) in clean_line(a[0], a[1], b[0], b[1]):
        _put(mat, step, part, x, y, m, s, p, overwrite)


def glyph_antler(ind, tubes, P, depth, size_px, front_right, mat, step, part, ti, max_h=None):
    """The small antler: beams leaning back ~45 deg over the neck (a vertical run reads as a
    horn, a pole, a giraffe's neck), no taller than the body is deep, with one tine pointing
    forward off each beam's middle at >= 10 px; a single dark pixel above the head at 6-7 px."""
    ant = ind.antler()
    if not ant:
        return
    am = rig.ANTLER if ant != "velvet" else rig.VELVET
    beams = [t for t in tubes if t.name.startswith(("beam", "ant"))]
    if not beams:
        return
    beams.sort(key=lambda t: depth(t.c.mean(0)))
    back = -1.0 if front_right else 1.0
    if size_px >= 8:
        for bt in beams[:1] if size_px < 11 else beams[:2]:
            pts = P(bt.c[[0, len(bt.c) // 2, -1]])
            base = pts[0]
            L = np.linalg.norm(pts[2] - base)
            if max_h is not None:
                L = min(L, max_h)
            L = max(L, 2.5)
            a = np.radians(48)
            tip = base + np.array([back * np.sin(a), -np.cos(a)]) * L
            mid = base + (tip - base) * 0.5 + np.array([back * 0.3, 0])
            _line(mat, step

# output:
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""imgfetch: fast, polite reference-image sourcing (Wikimedia Commons + Openverse).

  python3 imgfetch.py "cinder cone scoria slope" -n 12 -o refs/cinder
  python3 imgfetch.py -f queries.txt -n 8 -o refs            # one query per line; subdir per query
  options: --src commons,openverse   --width 1200   --sheet (writes <out>/_sheet.jpg)

Writes images plus <out>/sources.tsv (file, title, license, author, page url).
Cached: files already present are skipped. Handles 429/5xx with Retry-After backoff.
Parallel downloads (4 workers) with a shared per-host rate limiter (~3 req/s; Wikimedia's media
host is kept to 1 per 2 s and slows further on any 429, since it throttles hard per IP after bursts).
Openverse (mostly Flickr-hosted CC images) is the primary source because it stays fast.
"""
import argparse, concurrent.futures as cf, json, os, re, sys, threading, time, urllib.error, urllib.parse, urllib.request

UA = "pixelart-studies-imgfetch/1.0 (research; contact: local)"
_locks = {}; _last = {}; _glock = threading.Lock()

SLOW_HOSTS = {"upload.wikimedia.org": 2.0}   # Wikimedia media host: ~1 req/s or it 429s

def _throttle(host, min_gap=0.34):
    min_gap = SLOW_HOSTS.get(host, min_gap)
    with _glock:
        lk = _locks.setdefault(host, threading.Lock())
    with lk:
        dt = time.time() - _last.get(host, 0)
        if dt < min_gap:
            time.sleep(min_gap - dt)
        _last[host] = time.time()

def get(url, tries=4, timeout=25):
    host = urllib.parse.urlparse(url).netloc
    if host == "upload.wikimedia.org":
        tries = 2  # fail fast; other sources fill in
    for i in range(tries):
        _throttle(host)
        try:
            req = urllib.request.Request(url, headers={"User-Agent": UA})
            return urllib.request.urlopen(req, timeout=timeout).read()
        except urllib.error.HTTPError as e:
            if e.code == 429:
                with _glock:
                    SLOW_HOSTS[host] = min(8.0, SLOW_HOSTS.get(host, 0.34) * 2)  # adapt: this host wants us slower
            if e.code in (429, 500, 502, 503, 504) and i < tries - 1:
                wait = min(15, int(e.headers.get("Retry-After") or 0) or 2 * 2 ** i)
                time.sleep(wait); continue
            raise
        except (urllib.error.URLError, TimeoutError):
            if i < tries - 1:
                time.sleep(2 * (i + 1)); continue
            raise

def commons(query, n, width):
    q = urllib.parse.urlencode({"action": "query", "generator": "search", "gsrnamespace": 6, "gsrlimit": min(50, n * 3),
                                "gsrsearch": query + " filetype:bitmap", "prop": "imageinfo",
                                "iiprop": "url|size|extmetadata", "iiurlwidth": min((b for b in (330, 500, 960, 1280) if b >= width), default=1280), "format": "json"})
    d = json.loads(get("https://commons.wikimedia.org/w/api.php?" + q))
    out = []
    for p in sorted(d.get("query", {}).get("pages", {}).values(), key=lambda

Based on the search results, I found information about Ernest Thompson Seton's "Lives of Game Animals" and its availability on archive.org:

## About the Work

Volume I covers Cats, Wolves, and Foxes; Volume II covers Bears, Coons, Badgers, Skunks, and Weasels; Volume III covers Hoofed Animals; and Volume IV covers Rodents, Etc. The volumes include content about bears and cougars (mountain lions).

Ernest Thompson Seton wrote about the Cougar in his multivolume book series, Lives of Game Animals, describing it in detail within the work.

## Availability on Archive.org

The work is available on Internet Archive with a 1953 publication date from Charles T. Branford in Boston, containing 4 volumes in 8 parts with bibliographies. The archive.org link for Volume 3 is directly accessible, and other volumes should also be available through Internet Archive's collection.

The original work was published by Doubleday, Page & Company between 1925-1928 as the first edition, and has since been digitized and made available through archive.org for free access.

I've found the research paper you're looking for! Here are the key details:

**Publication Details:**
The study "Grizzly bear (Ursus arctos horribilis) locomotion: gaits and ground reaction forces" was published in the Journal of Experimental Biology in 2015, volume 218, pages 3102-3109. The authors include Catherine L. Shine from the University of Idaho, Charles T. Robbins and O. Lynne Nelson from Washington State University, and Craig P. McGowan.

**Study Overview:**
This study compared the locomotion of adult grizzly bears, including stride parameters, gaits and analysis of three-dimensional ground reaction forces, with that of previously studied quadrupeds.

**Key Findings:**
At slow to moderate speeds, grizzly bears use walks, running walks and canters. Vertical ground reaction forces demonstrated the typical M-shaped curve for walks with a significantly more pronounced pattern in the hindlimb, and the rate of force development was significantly higher for the hindlimbs than for the forelimbs at all speeds.

The full paper is accessible through the Journal of Experimental Biology website (DOI: 10.1242/jeb.121806).

Based on the search results, I found some relevant information about Muybridge's Animal Locomotion plates in the 720-750 range:

**Specific Plates Found:**

- Plate 720: Cat running
- Plate 730: Tiger Walking (1887)
- Plate 734: Two elephant walking
- Plate 737: Bactrian camel walking
- Plate 745: Raccoon walking and turning around
- Plate 748: Baboon walking on all fours

The search results don't provide a complete comprehensive list of all plates from 720-750, but they do reference several animals including cat, tiger, camels, raccoon, and baboon in that range. For a more detailed and complete catalogue of all plates in this specific range, you may want to consult the original "Prospectus and Catalogue of Plates" from 1887, which is available through the University of Pennsylvania Archives.

Bash: Download Muybridge catalogue and grep felid/bear plates
cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/bear-cougar/refs; mkdir -p lit; cd lit; curl -sL -o muybridge_catalogue.pdf https://archives.upenn.edu/wp-content/uploads/2018/01/muybridge-animal-locomotion-catalogue.pdf; ls -la; pdftotext -layout muybridge_catalogue.pdf cat.txt && grep -n -i -E "lion|tiger|cat|puma|cougar|bear|jaguar|leopard|raccoon|dog" cat.txt | head -60

Bash: Download Muybridge plates in background
mkdir -p ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/bear-cougar/refs/muybridge; cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/bear-cougar/refs/muybridge; cat > get.py <<'EOF'
import urllib.request, urllib.parse, time, os
UA="pixelart-studies-refs/1.0 (research)"
for n in [716,717,718,719,720,721,722,723,724,727,728,729,730,731,744,745]:
    fn=f"plate{n}.jpg"
    if os.path.exists(fn) and os.path.getsize(fn)>50000: continue
    name=f"Animal locomotion. Plate {n} (Boston Public Library).jpg"
    url="https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/Special:FilePath/"+urllib.parse.quote(name)+"?width=2400"
    for t in range(4):
        try:
            d=urllib.request.urlopen(urllib.request.Request(url,headers={"User-Agent":UA}),timeout=60).read()
            open(fn,"wb").write(d); print(n,len(d),flush=True); break
        except Exception as e:
            print(n,"err",e,flush=True); time.sleep(10*(t+1))
    time.sleep(4)
EOF
nohup python3 get.py > get.log 2>&1 &
sleep 1; tail -3 ../fetch.log

Two medium-sized males taken by Roosevelt in Colorado weighed 164 
and 160 lbs. A very large male which he secured was only 8 ft. in total 
length, though it weighed 227 lbs. For long this stood as the record, but 
recently a much larger one of probably 300 lbs. has been taken at Hillside near 
Prescott, Ariz. It was killed by J. К. Patterson, March, 1917. 

Concerning this monster, E. W. Nelson, Chief of the Biological Survey, 
writes me, Aug. 3, 1923: “А letter from M. E. Musgrave contains the follow- 
ing information: ‘After the Lion had been killed and skinned, I made a trip 
to Prescott where I was told of the weight of this Lion. Becoming interested 
I asked the express agent and several of the citizens of Prescott regarding the 
weight of the Lion, and they said he had been shipped in from Kirkland to 
Prescott by express, and that he weighed 276 lbs. after the intestines had 
been removed. 

* "The only measurements I got on the Lion were taken by a man in 
Prescott and by J. В. Patterson, the man who killed the Ілоп. He measured 
8 ft. 73 in. from the ар of his nose to the tip of his tail. 

**T have found since beginning our work in Arizona tha: Lions run 
much heavier in Arizona and New Mexico than they do in the northwestern 
part of the United States that I am familiar with. This, however, I believe, 
is the largest Mountain Lion that has ever been killed in this State."

The Blackbear has the characteristics of the genus as set forth already, 
and is further distinguished by the short-curved claws on its front feet, 
and the nearly straight profile of the skull. Besides these, its peculiarities 
of dentition, size, and colour are very distinctive.

wild one climb a tree. І had pictured to myself something like the slow 
moving up of a man or a Sloth, or at best, the action of an expert sailor going 
up the shrouds. But what I really saw, was more like the action of a Cat. 
It had not the spring and agility of a Squirrel or Marten, but this Bear 
went up twice as fast as any man could, and quite as well as any Monkey

In coming down, he travels tail first, whereas the Coon and Squirrel - 
come down head first. 

The following note by R. N. Pemberton, gives fuller details of the 
Bear's behaviour aloft, and also shows how he gets a brief respite when 
overharassed by Dogs: 

“The tree which the Dog was guarding so faithfully, was a large fir, 
and there was the Bear lying on his back across two limbs 80 feet from the 
ground. He was wagging his head from side to side and gasping for breath. 

“Tt is an interesting sight to see a Bear climb one of these large trees. 
He does not climb straight up, but circles around the body of the tree with 
a series of leaps, ascending corkscrew fashion until he reaches a convenient 
limb. 

* When he descends, he usually falls, landing upon his haunches and 
springing like a bounding ball out into the under-brush. Sometimes a 
Bear climbs a tree for a short distance, rests awhile, slides down and renews 
the chase. When he does this, the chase generally covers many miles.

Bash: Index plate pages in Seton PDFs
cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/bear-cougar/refs/lit; for f in livesofgameanima0000erne_l3y9 livesofgameanima0001erne; do echo "== $f"; pdfinfo $f.pdf | grep Pages; n=$(pdfinfo $f.pdf | awk '/Pages/{print $2}'); for p in $(seq 1 $n); do t=$(pdftotext -f $p -l $p $f.pdf - 2>/dev/null | tr '\n' ' '); case "$t" in *PLATE*|*Plate*) echo "$p: $(echo "$t" | grep -o -i 'PLATE[^.]\{0,70\}' | head -2 | tr '\n' '|')";; esac; done > ${f}_plates.txt; wc -l ${f}_plates.txt; done

WebFetch: https://journals.biologists.com/jeb/article/218/19/3102/14189/Grizzly-bear-Ursus-arctos-horribilis-locomotion
https://journals.biologists.com/jeb/article/218/19/3102/14189/Grizzly-bear-Ursus-arctos-horribilis-locomotion
Extract all quantitative gait data: gaits used and their speed ranges, duty factors, limb phase values, stride length/frequency vs speed, footfall sequences, any description of bear posture during walk (plantigrade, pigeon-toed, head movement), and comparisons to other quadrupeds. Include table values verbatim where possible.

# result:
# Grizzly Bear Locomotion: Quantitative Gait Data

## Speed Ranges and Gaits Used

Grizzly bears employed three primary gaits across the measured speed range of 1.1–3.8 m/s (Froude number: 0.42–1.46):

- **Walk**: 1.1–2.0 m/s (Fr₀.₅ = 0.42–0.77)
- **Running walk**: 2.0–3.0 m/s (Fr₀.₅ = 0.77–1.33)
- **Canter**: >3.0 m/s (Fr₀.₅ = 1.15+)

Only two gallops were recorded and excluded from analysis due to insufficient sample size.

## Duty Factor Data

For walks, duty factor decreased with speed following: *y* = −0.12*x* + 0.76 (R² = 0.65, P < 0.001). Running walks showed: *y* = −0.07*x* + 0.63 (R² = 0.53, P < 0.001). Canters displayed no significant speed relationship (*y* = 0.0083*x* + 0.37, R² = 0.02, P > 0.05). Walk-to-run transition occurred at Fr₀.₅ = 0.77, higher than horses at Fr₀.₅ = 0.55–0.61.

## Contact and Stride Time

Walking contact time: *y* = −0.38*x* + 1.2 (R² = 0.89). Stride time decreased significantly with speed across all gaits. "Swing time was independent of speed across gaits."

## Force Distribution

Forelimbs supported 54–60% of body weight, increasing with speed. Braking impulse for forelimbs exceeded hindlimbs at all speeds. Propulsive impulses were equal between limbs.

## Mediolateral Forces

Peak medial forces averaged approximately 14% of peak vertical force. These forces "were approximately equal to the absolute magnitude of the anterior–posterior forces," substantially exceeding typical cursorial quadrupeds (~5% of vertical force).

## Vertical Ground Reaction Forces

Walking produced characteristic M-shaped curves, "significantly more pronounced in the hindlimb." Rate of force development was significantly higher in hindlimbs (11.6±4.8 N/s⁻¹) versus forelimbs (6.3±4.0 N/s⁻¹).

## Posture Description

Bears exhibited "medially directed forefoot position during stance" and unusual carpal movement involving "medial rotation during swing." Researchers noted substantial "long axis rotation resulting in a 'waddle' from side to side."

## Key Comparison to Other Quadrupeds

Unlike digitigrade and unguligrade species, bears did not use trotting. Instead, "at intermediate speeds, bears used a running walk, as opposed to the more common trot."
WebFetch: https://sicb.org/abstracts/black-bear-ursus-americanus-gaits-and-ground-reaction-forces-a-comparison-to-grizzly-bears/
https://sicb.org/abstracts/black-bear-ursus-americanus-gaits-and-ground-reaction-forces-a-comparison-to-grizzly-bears/
Give the full abstract text, especially gaits used by black bears, speeds, and differences from grizzlies.

# result:
# Black Bear Locomotion Study Summary

## Full Abstract

Researchers studied three captive adult male black bears to compare their locomotion with previously studied grizzly bears. Both species share some biomechanical similarities but demonstrate key differences in gait selection and force production.

## Gaits and Speeds

Black bears employed different gaits depending on speed. At slow speeds, they "used a transverse walk" similar to grizzlies. However, "the gait used at intermediate speeds differs between the two species; grizzly bears use a running walk, while the black bears appeared to use a pace."

## Key Differences from Grizzlies

The most significant distinction involves lateral forces during movement. While grizzly bears "produced relatively high lateral forces during locomotion, up to 13% of the vertical force," black bears showed notably lower lateral forces, "below 6%." This suggests black bears don't use their limbs for propulsion identically to grizzlies.

Researchers noted that "some of the unusual results from the grizzly bears are not present in the black bears," indicating that distinctive grizzly locomotor characteristics aren't universal across bear species.

## Conclusion

The study indicates that unusual aspects of grizzly bear biomechanics appear species-specific rather than family-wide, necessitating additional research across Ursidae to fully understand bear locomotion.

THE ANATOMY, SIZE AND PROPORTIONS OF THE CAT. 
Plate XXV. 


Tue anatomy of the Cat bears a general resemblance to that of the Dog. The differences are 
chiefly matters of degree, as will be seen on comparing Plates VIII. and XXV. There are, however, 
some positive differences which should be kept in mind,—thus, all Cats have a clavicle, or collar- bone, 
in each shoulder, it is small but morphologically important; on the spine of the scapula is a process, 
called the metacromion, that is like a second acromion; and the claws with their sheaths, as well as 
the muscles which govern them, are highly developed so as to be sharp, recurved, and eminently 
retractile weapons, The whole skeleton is more elastic and better jointed, although the rest of the 
bones are similar to those of the Dog. 

The Cat has also more and better developed muscles. Thus, in the fore-leg the supinator

four varieti 
covered wit 


‘ ; ; Lion, with 
longus is far from being rudimentary; in the hind-leg it has the soleus; and according to Mivart, ‘iis Cat 1 
it has on the breast a second sternalis. niente 


The shoulder has so much play that the scapula may touch the jaw, or slide back as far as the 
eighth or tenth rib; while the muscles regulating these movements are greatly developed, endowing the 
Cat with remarkable power for climbing and for striking blows. 

But these differences, it will be seen, are secondary matters, and would not impair the truth of 
the broad statement, that in their Art Anatomy the Dog and the Cat are identical.

THE COUGAR, PUMA, PANTHER, OR MOUNTAIN LION. 
(Felis concolor.) 


Tuis is simply a large Cat of extraordinary agility and suppleness. In all its proportions and anatomy, 
it agrees with the Cat. 

When young it is variegated, like most of the Felide, with black spots on the body, and rings on the 
tail. But these markings disappear as it matures, in order, it is supposed, to make it more like the Deer 
upon which it preys. 

Audubon and Bachman give the following measurements :—

CHAPTER XXIII. 


THE CHEETAH, CHITA, OR HUNTING LEOPARD. 
(Cynailurus jubatus.) 


Tuis remarkable animal, although classed with the Cats, has somewhat of the Dog in its form, 
anatomy, habits, and proportions, It is long-legged, going in a square of three heads; its paws are but 
little prehensile, its claws are blunt and non-retractile, and it cannot climb. It is the species which 
properly bears the name ‘ Leopard,’ as it was supposed by the ancients to be a hybrid between a Lion 
and a Pard or Panther; but the present allotment of names is according to modern usage. 

Mr. Blanford says, ‘It is probable that for a short distance the Hunting Leopard is the swiftest of 
all mammals.’

Bash: Crawl Commons categories for bear and puma
pkill -f imgfetch.py; cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/bear-cougar/refs; cat > cats.py <<'EOF'
import json, urllib.request, urllib.parse, time, sys
UA="pixelart-studies-refs/1.0 (research; bear-cougar study)"
def api(**p):
    p.update(format="json")
    u="https://commons.wikimedia.org/w/api.php?"+urllib.parse.urlencode(p)
    for t in range(5):
        try: return json.loads(urllib.request.urlopen(urllib.request.Request(u,headers={"User-Agent":UA}),timeout=30).read())
        except Exception as e: time.sleep(5*(t+1))
    return {}
def members(cat, typ):
    out=[];cont={}
    while True:
        d=api(action="query",list="categorymembers",cmtitle=cat,cmtype=typ,cmlimit=500,**cont)
        out+= [m["title"] for m in d.get("query",{}).get("categorymembers",[])]
        if "continue" in d: cont={"cmcontinue":d["continue"]["cmcontinue"]}; time.sleep(1)
        else: break
    return out
root=sys.argv[1]
seen=set(); todo=[(root,0)]; files=[]
while todo:
    c,dep=todo.pop(0)
    if c in seen: continue
    seen.add(c)
    fs=members(c,"file"); files+= [(c,f) for f in fs]
    if dep<2:
        for s in members(c,"subcat"): todo.append((s,dep+1))
    time.sleep(0.7)
    print(c,len(fs),file=sys.stderr)
for c,f in files: print(c+"\t"+f)
EOF

# output:
# Roosevelt elk and black-tailed deer, after Mark Ferrari: lessons

A student's log. Subject: Roosevelt elk (bull, cow, yearling, calf) and Columbian black-tailed
deer (buck, doe, fawn), as individuals and as herds, from a 60 px hero animal down to a 4 px
figure and a 2 px dot, in the landscape view (camera 6-10 deg) and the 30 deg iso view.

Everything here is made by the programs in `src/`. Stage images are in `stages/`, parameter
sheets and animations in `sheets/`, photos in `refs/` (fetched with the studio's imgfetch;
licences in each `sources.tsv`) plus the naturalist's `~/work/naturalist/animals/photos/`.



cd src
nice -n 10 python3 sheets.py            # sheets A-H (~5 min, 4 processes)
nice -n 10 python3 anim.py              # gait and herd GIFs
nice -n 10 python3 context.py           # the two context pieces
python3 s0_copy.py                      # the stage-0 master copy




The technique as code: one call paints one animal, one more paints a herd.



import rig, paint, herd
from render import View
ind = rig.Individual(&quot;elk&quot;, &quot;m&quot;, &quot;adult&quot;, season=&quot;autumn&quot;, seed=2)     # species, sex, age, season, seed
R, tubes, k = paint.paint_index(ind, gait=&quot;trot&quot;, phase=0.3,           # stand graze walk trot gallop
                                view=View(yaw=-14, elev=8,              #   alert bugle lie bound stot
                                          sun_az=-150, sun_el=30, diffuse=0),
                                size_px=48)                             # body length in px
rgb = paint.colour(R, ind, hour=&quot;golden&quot;, ground=&quot;autumn&quot;, depth=0.0)   # the palette is the light
img = herd.scene(herd.elk_herd(&quot;graze&quot;, 26, seed=7), k=6, W=320, H=180, elev=30, hour=&quot;morning&quot;)




`R.mat` / `R.step` are the index canvas (material, step 0-5); the palette maps them to colour for
an hour, season and depth, so one canvas serves every time of day.

| module | what it is |
|---|---|
| `rig.py` | the animal: species proportions (torso lofted from photo stations), sex/age/season/seed variation, poses and gaits (IK legs), antlers |
| `render.py` | tubes -> supersampled surface splats -> z-buffer, light-space shadow, pixel reduction; thin parts redrawn as clean runs |
| `coat.py` | hide regions in pose-free body coordinates: the elk's cape, rump patch, dark belly, face; deer throat, forehead, black tail, ears; fawn spots |
| `hide.py` | pixel rules from the Ferrari copy: plane flattening, edge bands, lit edges, back-light rim, hair-dash dither |
| `fringe.py` | fur edges: interlocking teeth between hide colours, the bull's hanging mane |
| `micro.py` | the glyph painter under 10 px, and the small antler used up to 16 px |
| `palette.py` | (material, step) -> colour per coat, hour, season and depth |
| `herd.py` | herd arrangements from the naturalist's guide, and the compositor |
| `ground.py` | a calm meadow to stand on (grass-lod owns grass; this is only a backdrop) |
| `sheets.py`, `anim.py`, `context.py` | the deliverables |
| `board.py`, `size

Bash: Contact sheet for Seton Art Anatomy
cd /tmp/seton; L=~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/bear-cougar/refs/lit; nice pdftoppm -r 14 -gray $L/cihm_35627.pdf a; python3 - <<'EOF'
from PIL import Image, ImageDraw
import glob
fs=sorted(glob.glob('/tmp/seton/a-*.pgm'))
ims=[Image.open(f) for f in fs]
w=max(i.width for i in ims);h=max(i.height for i in ims);cols=22
for s in range(0,len(ims),110):
    chunk=ims[s:s+110]; rows=(len(chunk)+cols-1)//cols
    sheet=Image.new('L',(cols*(w+3),rows*(h+12)),255);d=ImageDraw.Draw(sheet)
    for k,(im,f) in enumerate(zip(chunk,fs[s:s+110])):
        x=(k%cols)*(w+3);y=(k//cols)*(h+12);sheet.paste(im,(x,y+11));d.text((x,y),f.split('-')[-1][:-4],fill=0)
    sheet.save(f'/tmp/seton/sheet_a_{s}.png');print(sheet.size)
EOF

Bash: Combine anatomy plates into one image
cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/bear-cougar/refs/lit/seton_plates; python3 -c "
from PIL import Image
ims=[Image.open(f'anat_p{p}-{p}.png').convert('L') for p in (152,154,156,158)]
h=max(i.height for i in ims); W=sum(i.width for i in ims)

# output:
"""A calm meadow to stand the animals on (not the subject of this study; grass-lod owns grass).

Ferrari's middle-distance grass is a checker of 2-3 close greens with the form turned by the
density of the lighter one (SYNTHESIS, grass-lod stage 0). So: value noise at a few metres
decides the local density of the lighter step; a 2x2 Bayer turns density into pixels; near
the bottom of a landscape view, sparse vertical 1x2 ticks of the darker step. Everything in
(material GROUND, step) so the hour palette lights it.
"""
import numpy as np
from scipy import ndimage as ndi

BAYER = np.array([[0, 2], [3, 1]])


def value_noise(shape, cell, rng):
    h, w = shape
    gh, gw = h // cell + 3, w // cell + 3
    g = rng.random((gh, gw))
    return ndi.zoom(g, cell, order=3)[:h, :w]


def meadow(shape, k, seed=0, elev=8.0, base=3.0, contrast=0.9, ticks=True):
    """Returns an int step array (0..5) for a meadow of `shape`, k px per metre."""
    rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)
    h, w = shape
    cell = int(np.clip(k * 3.0, 3, 40))
    n = value_noise(shape, cell, rng) * 0.65 + value_noise(shape, max(3, cell // 3), rng) * 0.35
    x = base - 0.5 + (n - 0.5) * 2 * contrast + 0.5
    yy, xx = np.mgrid[0:h, 0:w]
    t = (BAYER[yy % 2, xx % 2] + 0.5) / 4
    fl = np.floor(x)
    st = (fl + ((x - fl) > t)).astype(np.int32)
    if ticks and k >= 6:
        # near grass: sparse vertical ticks of the darker step (minority marks in a lit mass)
        dens = 0.018 * np.clip(k / 20, 0.3, 1.2) * (x < base + 0.1)
        m = rng.random(shape) < dens
        L = int(np.clip(round(k / 16), 1, 2))
        for j in range(L + 1):
            mm = np.roll(m, j, axis=0)
            st[mm] = np.minimum(st[mm], np.floor(x[mm]).astype(np.int32) - 1)
    return np.clip(st, 0, 5)

"""Black bear and cougar as 3-D rigs: species proportions, pose, gait -> a list of tubes.

Units are shoulder heights (H = 1) until the end of build(), then metres. Axes: x forward, y left,
z up; the animal stands on z = 0 with its withers near x = 0.2 (bear) / 0.35 (cougar).

The model system is Ernest Thompson Seton's (Studies in the Art Anatomy of Animals, 1896; Lives
of Game Animals, 1925-26): an animal measured in head-lengths, the bear "in a square of two
heads" with the arch of the back rising above it, the cat "in a rectangle 2 1/2 heads high by
3 1/2 long", tail about half the total length; checked, number by number, against photos on the
comparison board (board.py).

The difference that matters most between the two is in the legs:

  - the bear is PLANTIGRADE: the whole hind foot, heel to toes, is on the ground, the forefoot's
    palm too, turned in (pigeon-toed); the legs are short thick columns under a deep barrel;
  - the cougar is DIGITIGRADE: it stands on its toes, the hock and wrist are well off the ground,
    the metapodials slope; the legs are long for a cat but the body is long and low.
So a leg is: root (shoulder or hip joint, in the body) -> upper bone (humerus / femur) -> IK
joint (elbow bends back, knee bends forward) -> lower bone (radius / tibia) -> wrist or ankle ->
metapodial at a pose-given angle -> digits at a pose-given angle. The stance angles are the
species: a bear's hind metapodial lies flat (angle 84 deg from vertical), a cougar's slopes
(24 deg).
"""
import numpy as np
from dataclasses import dataclass, field, replace

# material ids (coat regions). The painter maps each to a ramp.
(BODY, DARK, PALE, BELLY, LEG, CLAW, SPOT, TIP, NOSE, EARIN, THROAT, TAILTIP, MUZZLE, BLAZE, EYE,
 FACE) = range(16)
MAT_NAMES = ["body", "dark", "pale", "belly", "leg", "claw", "spot", "tip", "nose", "earin",
             "throat", "tailtip", "muzzle", "blaze", "eye", "face"]


@dataclass
class Tube:
    name: str
    c: np.ndarray        # (n,3) centreline
    a: np.ndarray        # (n,) radius along up-normal
    b: np.ndarray        # (n,) radius along lateral
    up: np.ndarray       # (3,) reference up for framing
    mat: object
    thin: bool = False
    cap0: bool = True
    cap1: bool = True
    width_px_min: float = 1.0


# ---------------------------------------------------------------- species

@dataclass
class Species:
    name: str
    H: float                     # shoulder height, metres (adult female; males scaled in spec())
    torso: list                  # stations (x, ztop, zbot, halfwidth)
    neck_base: tuple             # (x, z)
    neck_len: float
    neck_ang: float              # standing neck angle above horizontal
    neck_r0: tuple
    neck_r1: tuple
    head_len: float              # occiput to nose
    head_ang: float              # standing head axis (negative = nose down)
    head_r0: tuple               # cranium (a, b)
    head_r1: tuple               # muzzle (a, b)
    muzzle: float                # fraction of the head that is muzzle
    ear_len: float
    ear_w: float
    fore_root: tuple             # shoulder joint (x, z, y)
    hind_root: tuple             # hip joint (x, z, y)
    fore_len: tuple              # humerus, radius, metacarpal, digits
    hind_len: tuple              # femur, tibia, metatarsal, digits
    fore_r: tuple                # upper arm, forearm, metacarpal, paw
    hind_r: tuple                # thigh, shank, metatarsal, paw
    fore_meta: float             # stance metacarpal angle from vertical, forward + (deg)
    hind_meta: float
    tail_len: float
    tail_r: float
    scap_top: tuple = (0.3, 0.95)   # top of the scapula (x, z) standing
    toe_in: float = 0.0          # forepaw rotation inward (deg): the bear's pigeon toes
    fur: float = 0.0             # silhouette shag (px teeth at hero size)


BEAR_TORSO = [
    # x      ztop   zbot   hw    black bear, side views: a deep round barrel, the back rising from
    # the shoulders to the highest point over the hips, the croup falling steeply to a stub tail,
    # the belly low and shaggy, the legs short
    (0.52, 0.80, 0.56, 0.10),    # brisket, forward of the forelegs
    (0.46, 0.93, 0.47, 0.18),
    (0.34, 0.985, 0.42, 0.23),   # chest
    (0.20, 1.00, 0.405, 0.26),   # withers over the forelegs: no hump
    (0.04, 0.995, 0.395, 0.28),
    (-0.12, 1.005, 0.39, 0.29),  # barrel, belly low
    (-0.28, 1.03, 0.41, 0.28),
    (-0.42, 1.05, 0.44, 0.26),   # the rump: the highest point of the back
    (-0.54, 1.025, 0.49, 0.23),
    (-0.63, 0.95, 0.55, 0.17),
    (-0.69, 0.85, 0.61, 0.10),   # buttock, round
    (-0.71, 0.75, 0.66, 0.04),
]

COUGAR_TORSO = [
    # cougar: long and low, a deep narrow chest, the belly rising to the flank and a loose skin
    # pouch hanging in front of the stifle, the croup a touch higher than the withers (the hind
    # legs are longer), the buttock round
    (0.66, 0.78, 0.60, 0.06),    # front of the chest, point of the shoulder
    (0.60, 0.90, 0.50, 0.12),
    (0.50, 0.97, 0.465, 0.15),
    (0.35, 1.00, 0.45, 0.165),   # withers over the girth
    (0.18, 0.975, 0.46, 0.175),
    (0.00, 0.96, 0.485, 0.18),
    (-0.18, 0.965, 0.52, 0.165),
    (-0.34, 0.985, 0.565, 0.145),  # loin, flank tuck
    (-0.48, 1.01, 0.535, 0.15),  # the primordial pouch sags here
    (-0.60, 1.005, 0.585, 0.15), # croup
    (-0.70, 0.955, 0.635, 0.12),
    (-0.77, 0.875, 0.69, 0.08),  # buttock
    (-0.80, 0.80, 0.73, 0.03),
]

SPECIES = {
    "bear": Species(

        neck_base=(0.40, 0.76), neck_len=0.30, neck_ang=8.0, neck_r0=(0.27, 0.23), neck_r1=(0.19, 0.17),
        head_len=0.44, head_ang=-22.0, head_r0=(0.155, 0.15), head_r1=(0.068, 0.062), muzzle=0.5,

        fore_meta=78.0, hind_meta=84.0, tail_len=0.09, tail_r=0.035,

        ear_len=0.085, ear_w=0.065,
